London.—Through the failure of 
the electric light supply at West 
"London County Court recently, 
Judge Hargreaves finished his work 
by candlelight. On the. previous

The vision of a great Empire net-- 
work of airship and airplane: ser-, 
vices, bringing the: remotest parts 
of the Empire’ within a few days’ 
flight of London, may soon be 
realised, ji 

Ten years of flying on ‘cross- 
Channe! air, routes has resulted in 
the accumulation of vast stores of 
experience in the commercial. operae 


tion of aircraft. 


Luxurtous gabins. 

From the 360-h.p. converted bamb- 
ing planes, carrying only two--pas- | 
sengers ina cramped space covered 
by a lid which was shut down’ after 
the passengers had climbed in, the 
giant triplescrew air liners, with 


| their 1,200-h.p. engines and Juxur- 


ious cabins, carrying a ateward and 
a buffet to cater for the material 
comfort of the twenty passengers, 
have been evolved. 

Tho original’ 225, milea of. airway 
between London and Paris has been 
extended until it now stretches, 
over sens and deserts, 5,000 miles to 
India, and ‘operates with clockwork 
regularly, 

10,000 Miles More, 

A. further 10,000 miles is shortly 
to be added to’ this great Empire, 
air route, by the opening of exten- 
sions across India, through the East 


Indies, to Australia, and by, a great 
trunk line through’ Africa from 


| Criro to Capetown. 


To. realise this dream of an Em- | 
| pire ir” network, however, great 
dcéans must be crossed. Experts, 
therefore, have turned their atten- 
tian to tho problem of utilising 
lighter-than-air craft for the trana- 
ocean links of the Empire air, net- 
work. 


There ‘could be no half measures 
with airships for this purpose, no 
gradual’ development such as the 
air liner is now undergoing. It was 
a-big jump from the small military 


comfortable ‘accommodation for the 
crew, to great Juxury craft . Jike’ 
R101, 


Enthusiasm for air communica- 
tions is greater in the Dominions 
than here at home.’ Canada and 
South Africa have already erected 
‘airship mooring marta to weleome 
the new airships. " 


The British Government have 
built ‘airship bases in. Egypt and 
India. 

The British Empire is to be link- 
ed together by a net-work of the 
air lines, operited by both airships 
and airplanes, which will quicken 
communication, and therefore trade 
not ‘only 
and the Motherland, but also be- 
tween Dominion and Dominion.

PROBLEMS OF 
_ EMPIRE. 


DEFINITE TEST OF 
STABILITY WITHIN 
NEXT DECADE. 


“Mr W, T. Sanders, MB, presid- 
‘od at the Fabian Socicty’s mecting 
in Kingsway Hall last month when 
Mr, 6. K,. Rateliffe delivered a 
lecture on ‘Empire or the Com- 
monwealth.”’ , 

Mr. Ratcliffe, in. his brilliant 
survey of the fundainental changes 
that are taking place in the great 
British association of countrics, 
and of the “problems immediately | 
ahead began with a glance at ‘the| 
old conceptions of empire. 

The Victorian assumption, far too 
easy, he anid, wag that the threefold 
division of the British system into: 
free dominions, crown colonies, and 
India, would continue virtually un- 
changed, and that the course of 
evolution ‘would “not be subject to 
any catastrophe, while our later 
theorists seemed to take for granted 
that the “special providence which 
had so conspicuously. watched over 
Britain would ensure the clear de- 
velopment of this vast and varie-, 
gated system into the British com- 
monwealth of nations, tha, concept 
for” which ‘General Smuts‘ during 
the war did perhaps more than any 
other public man. 

* We knew to-day that the British 


wey system wag not and never could be 


simple. It had moved into 5 great 
er complexity, and the next: ten 
years would in all probability bring 
a definite test of its stability. The 
dominions themselvta comprised an 
immense variety of problems, The 
definition of:‘free nationhood and 
,equal partnérship made at the Im- 
perial Conference of 1926 marked a 
notable ‘stage in the commonwealth 
idea, but it was :a mistake to sup- 
pose either. that wo had arrived at 
a clenr conception of partnership, 
or that the problem of one dominion’ 
was the problem of all. 

Canada, for example, had its own 
social and geographical difficulties, 
complicated by .tho immensq, and 
continuous pressure of the United 
States. The Canaditn social sys- 
tem’ was established, ‘and the Com- 
monwealth tie was strony, The com- 
ing decade would show to what ex- 
tent Canada wag able to vesiat. the 
pull from the south in trade and in. 
chances of ‘work and rich rewards 
in the United States. 


The Australian problem, offered a 
great contrast to the Canadian. In 
some respects: the Australian cffort 
was the most interesting’ in. the 
world, for here a community only 
half as large again as tho people of 
Scotland .were attempting to built 
a closely protected egalitariam 
State on the fringe of a continent 
nearly as large ag the United 
States. “No question of the coming 
decade—not even that of South | 
Africa—was more interesting than 
the question whether the Australian 
idea could be worked out with or 
without the influx of a great labour 
force from Europe. 


. The Crown Colonies, 


The Crown, colonics, the Jecturer 
suggested, would continue to fur- 
nish the most attractive and satis 
factory examples of the British ¢ Bys- 
tem of administration and economic 
effort, for there the part performed 


large, and the rapidly maturing 
schemes of development ‘were im- 
"| menge in scope and certain of re- 
gulta. . 

It wong first in Kenya, and, next 
in India that the British systera’ 
would in all probability’ meet the 
crucial tests. Kenya afforded the 
most difficul, case for settling the | 
rights of native land ownership’ 
and the critical question of empire 
‘citizenship, while India was the 
most baffling prohlem, constitution- 
al-and social, ever grappled with 
‘under the authority of an imperial 
system. The epeed of India’s 
changes had been extraordinary. 
Between the old: India and the 
‘Indin which had. responded _- to 
Gandhi there was a gulf. which 
only the fincet statesmanship could 
hope to bridge. If in the next.ten 
yentg or the next two—for the 
margin of time was extremely short 
—the British genius could achieve 
a pacific transition the British. sys- 
tem would have justified itself.

: Almost’ indescribable injuries. 
against , a servant girl of her own

had Kot: van inkling that all was, 


not right at 297 Rue Vouillemont, 
and Detective Sub-Inspector Henri 
of the French Police, led a raid

ant barber-and his wife, a Chinese

world like an animal and'in a, 
terrible (condition. Promptly mak-

the barber and hig wife, and re 
moved the girl to hospital, 

Blave Girl's Misery.

treated her ever since.

At the Hospital Ste, Marie Dr. 
Allary, made o careful examination

sary to operate immediately on hey 
‘left knee, which was poisoned and 
swollen ‘up to a large size. The

‘dition, having been nearly clawed ° 
out, while the little finger of the

‘sharp instrument at‘its base, and- 
was hanging by! a fragment. of 
sinew. The girl has a hole in her~ 
scalp, while the whole body is cover- 
‘ed, with bruises and cuts, with dry 
blood in. several places. 

When -police investigations are

ed Court, while the child will most, 
probably: be sent to the Door of. 
Hope when she hag recovered. While

It was recently in London decid- 
ed im the Divoree Court that a 
husband’s answer to a wife's peti- 
conjugal 
rights, alleging ,” extravagance on 
her part, > was good’ in law, and 
that he had a right to work,wnere 
he like in order to: maintain ner, 

The case' was listed as G—, M., 
against G—, A.D., the identity of 
‘thy parties not bejng revealed, ‘The 
husband alleged that he allowed 
his wife £2,400 a year, and that-she 
failed to live within her allowahee, 

He also contended (that he was | 

ysible to. live in this country be- 
cause his position as a partner, in 
i in of tea merchants, operating 
in England and India, required 
that he should live ‘in India, He 
niso said that if he had his wife’) 
to live with him in India he was 
threatened with the loss of his 
position. 

The -husbend's income wag stated, 
to be £11,000 a year, | 

Debts Paid O02, 

Mr. Justice Hill, giving judg- 
ment, said that the husband as- 
serted that his wife largely exceed- 
ed the wlowance of more than 
£2,400 a year which Fe made to 
her, and incurred heavy debts, “He 
paid them off in 1020, 1925, and 
1927 to the ‘amount‘of £10,000, 

The husband alleged that she in- 
curred further debts in 192$ to the 
amonnt ‘of about £7,000. 

Mr. "Justice Hil added: ‘that if 
the facts mentioned by the husband 
were proved, the husband would 
show 2 reasonable cause for refus- 
ing to have his wife with hifh in 
India. and it would he) contrary 
to'the real truth of the case to 
treat ag ‘deserting his wife n hus- 
band who, in these circumstances, 
refused to have his wife to live 
with him in India. 

'A husband is bound to _main- 
tain his wife and child and: to 
earn his living,” said Mr,’ Justice 
Hill. ‘Tt 1g for him to choose how 
d whero he shall.do that.’ If his 
choice is Renuine T know not on 
what grounds it can be said, to be 
a breach. of duty to the wife.”

“The Society of St. George exists:for the purpose of streng- 


thening the sentiment. of English nationality, . . . 


is the representative of individual 
the spirit of St, George. 


George ‘did not sit.on a commitice and vote for somebody ‘else. to

+ action. 
Twelfth Night Ball, 


held for tho first time this year in the 


Peninsula Hotel, was a typically English gathering. The Rose Room 
was, of course, the more important of the two big dance halls and 
an admirable effect was achioved by the use of rose and amber 
lights, Attention was at once drawn to the imposing figure of Str 
George, flanked by big standards with the red cross on a white 
field, which stood behind the dais. Tho excellent’ arrangements of 

~ by-pass exits from. both sections of the ball room, prevented tho 
uncomfortable crowding in the Bottle neck between the two halls , 
which to some extent spoilt the pleasantnesa of St. Andrew's | 


} Ball. . 


THE ‘PIG AND ‘WHISTLE. 


» “The “ Pig and Whistle,” outside which was hung a cleverly © 
éxecuted Inn signboard did a #ood trade, and the atmosphere of : 
good fellowship which. prevailed behind its doors spoke eloquently 
of the great qualities which have made the Inn so fine and typical 


apart of English village life. 


The gay company of brave men and fair Jadies who thronged.. - 
the dance halls, in conjunction with the excellent decoration of the 
rooms, carried the minds of many of those preeent back to the 
hunt balls of the Shires, and for every one there were pleasant 
reminiscences'of the Home land ‘to be conjured up by a glance 
around the walls, where the crests of English towns flanked by, fags 


‘made a pleasing display of heraldic colouring.

PRESIDENT’S SPEECH. 


“Addressing the assembly, the 
President said:— : 
Your Excellencies, Ladies and 
Gentlemen,—It is my privilege to- 
night ‘to propose the toast of ‘! St. 
George and England'’—o toast 
that goes straight to the heart of

full the great advantage conferred 


carries with it. i

V.C. “DIES: FROM’ GRIEF. 
_UNABLE TO ATTEND’ THB 
PRINCE’S DINNER. 
Sergeant-Major John. Crawshaw 
Raynes, V.C., died nt his home in 
Leeds a few wecks ogo, oa 
He had grieved. greatly because 
he was unable to accept the invita- 


tion of the Prince of Wales to at-' 


tend the V.C.s! dinner in the House 


"| of Lords .in November owing to the 


fact that he was bedridden—and 
had been for three and n half years 
in consequence of a war wound. * 

Sergeant Major Raynes was.a 


Force. mis 


He won his V.C. at Fosse de Be- 
thune in Octuber 1915, when he was ° 
w sergeant in the Royal Field Ar- .- 


tillery. 


' Hé went out under intense shell 


fire to the assistance of m wounded 
‘sergeant until recalled to his gun, 
but-when ‘cease fire’ was ordered 


he’ returned to the wounded man, 


and. carried him to o dug-cut, 
Buried In Cellar, 

A gas shell burst ot the mouth 
of tho dug-out, and Sergeant-Ma- 
jor Raynes: once more ran..acroas 
the open, ‘fetched his own smoke 


helmet, put it on his comrade, and: 


then, ‘himself badly gassed, stag- 
gared back to his gun. 


The following day, at’ Quality , 
Street, Sergeant-Major Raynes was ” 


‘buried in’ the cellar of a house and 


wounded in the'hend and leg, but - 


when' rescued he insisted on remain- 


ing: uhder heavy;'shell fire to assist - 


in the rescue of the other men:

Washington, Dec. 30.—President 
Hoover is prepared to introduce 
the report of his Law Enforcement 
Commission, headed by George 
Wickersham, before Congress in 10

COLLEGE ATHLETICS IN — 
; US. ° 
PLEA FOR LESS SEMI- © 
PROFESSIONALISM. * 


New York, January 1.—Propos- 
tls designed to oppose the current 


trend of college athletics toward | 


commercialism were introduced ,by 
Dr. Frank Day, president ‘of 
Union College, at ‘the opening of 
the convention of the National 
Collegate' Athletic Association. 
. Hig suggestions were 08 follow: 

First, tho abolition of “gate re- 
ceipts’’ from college football and 
other games,” ae ; 

“Second, the abandonment of pro- 
fessional coaching, as well os of 
scouting, subsidizing and directing 
from the side-lines; all of which 
Dr. Day declared would aid to- 
ward “a return to truly amateur. 
athletics.”” .. : 

Third, the forming of 0. league 
among colleges desiring to engage 
in purely. amateur contests, _ 

Fourth, that college players 
should receiye no compensation 
whatever, in any form. , . 

Fifth, that athletica should in 
all respects be made clean.and open, 
giving every student an opportun- 
ity to participate rather than wéed- 
ing out a chosen few skilled athle- 


‘| tes to represent the jnstitution and. 


leaving the majority without athle- 
tic training. ae 

“T cannot hope, however.”’ Dr. 
Day concluded in rather mournful 
strain, “that these remarks of mine 
will bave much effect.” -

COMMENTS. 


“ (By: “ BROADOASTER.”] 

To a large section of the public, 
the most interesting feature of the 
sports .nows over ‘the week-end, ls 
undoubtedly the publication af 
training times in the columns of 
the Daily Press, T am gind it has 
brea. announced that this feature 
will, appear regularly ‘after the 
gallops, as this is the only connec- 
tion which a very large. proportion, 
of racegoers have with, the train- 
ing performances. A very small 
minority line ‘the rails in the 
mornings, and even to them the 


publication of. training times has, 


some interest; as it helps, firstly to 
check their own observations, and 
secondly ta fill in gaps which they 
must necessarily, leave. I 'see that 


"Jno fewer than sixty, animals were 
| clocked ’” on Saturday,.and as the 


height of the training is approach- 
ed, more and more animals will be 
timed,’ making the feature still 
amore interesting. 
ra 

In spite of the “fact that there 
is’ still seven weeks to go before 
the races, gossip is already going 
round as to the possible Derby and 
Champions winners, For the Cham- 
pions, Apollo appears jnvincible in 
the eyes of a good many,’ and for 
the Derby, Mr. Ho Kom Tong looks 
as if he is going to repeat last 
year’s suceéss with one or other of 
his animals. Mr. ;Dunbar has ‘a 
long ‘string of, animals, to select 
from, and will certainly be much 
in the limelight during the training 
season. Mr. Chan Tin Sion has an 
excellent chance of performing the 
double with Apollo.and Wisdom 
Stag, the latter being o useful-look- 
ing grey which moves very con- 
vincingly. é i

SUCCESS OF WIRE- 
LESS “LIGHTHOUSE.” 


— 


GOOD REPORTS FROM 
SHIPPING. ~ 


The Air Ministry ‘rotating wireless 
bencon at Oxford’ Ness, Suffolk, 
whichis being worked experimentally 
by the Air Ministry’ in conjunction 
with’ the Board of Trade, has proved 


1 6 sucdessful in enabling shipping 


to find its true bearings aut, of 


sight of land, that it is to be work." 


ed continudusly throughout the 24 


«hours for two months. 


‘Outwardly the beacon resembles a 
windmill without the sails and it is 
situated within a short distance of 
the Oxford Lighthouse, but inside 
it is ‘really o hollow tower shelter- 
ing 1 large frame acriil which 
makes one -complete revolution 
every minute and is electrically con- 
trolled to maintain absolute ac: 
curacy of time in rotation and: auto- 
natically to-send out certain signals 
at ‘known points’ of the compass. 
It is in-effect, an acrial- “ Tight- 
house,” “from which w wireless: in- 
stead of a light.beam is sent.out in 
such a way that during each revolu- 
tion of the beacon the signals re- 
ceived ‘by the observer rise and 
fall ‘in intensity: passing” through a 
maximum and: minimum twice in 
cach minute. The positions of 
minimum intensity wre very sharp 
and can be very accurately obser- 
so’ that,'they .are used for 
navigation purposes, and all ‘the 
mariner, or the aircraft ‘pilot, needs 
iy a ‘wireless receiving set and a 
stop-watch. 


Reports From Ships. 


most interesting 
have been received from’ ships, 
coastwise ‘steamers using 
the ‘ast Const, but a trawler’ cap- 
tain aff the west ‘const’ of Ireland 
has also reported picking up_ the 
beacon sufficiently well to establish 
his position, A fow' selections from: 
the reports to date are appepded':— 


Off Beachy Head, although 100 
ausiles from the beacon it came in 
‘within Ideg., from tho correct; re- 
‘ult. 2 

Signals very clear ‘und distinct. 
Bearings, within deg: at 78 and 

124 miles. 
Wireless: operator on ship re- 


Some reports 


_ ports that, minimum -intensity is‘ 
very clear ‘and sharp. 


ee During voynge from ‘Landon to 
Tyne’ a number of observations 
~ were ‘taken by wireless operator, 
and the captain afterwards report. 
ed same to be exact. i 
‘Observed bearing Sideg., esti- 
mated distance #3 miles, Presum- 
ing aceuracy’ of bearing’ given 
from the the wireless beacon, the 
vessel had 17 ‘miles run to the 
Crossand Light» Vessel. After 
arriving log registered 18 miles, 
.one mile out being estimated due 
to adverse tide. 
T should like to express my 
* earnest appreciation of the: ease 
and simplicity and apparent ac- 
curacy of this installation. 
Captainof a Dutch vessel thinks 
this method of obtaining W/T 


bearings ‘ the best¢known method 


” 


for use on board ships,” and men- 
tions that he has tried it against 
ordinary wireless Sn a eae 
apparatus, ' 


The beacon wns "evolved by the 
Royal ‘Aircraft Establishment at 
Farnborough, and ‘so far it has 
_ proved more useful to shipping than 
to aircraft, as nearly all commercial 
aireraft * alrendy have «their own 
specialized system of obtaining bear- 


\ings along the cross-Channel routes. 


Trinity House Report. , 

The Trinity House ‘observers ‘also 
give a, most favourable report, say- 
ing that out of 21 observations only 
five showed an error of one degree, 
while the National Physical Labora- 
tory, ‘which conducted tests aa to 
the accuracy of the time-keeping, re- 
ported’ the accuracy was well be- 


yond the limit of accuracy of the. 


ordinary chronometer or stop-watch, 
and the rotation of the beacon must 
therefore: be taken as 60 seconds 
exactly. 


Naturally much depen upon the 


ability of the observer to ‘uso his 
stop-watch exactly, and the watch 


| can only register time in fifths of 


a. second, but it seems certain that, 
he ‘ore experience is gained by 
‘mariners, so the exactitude of the 
bearings obtained will increase. 
One ship’s master. reported that the 
beacon will be most useful to him in 
fog, especially when bound south or 
making for the Enst Goodwins Light- 
ship, and should further experience 
show ‘that shipping traffic appre- 
ciates this new aid to accurate navi- 


gation this station may become per-’ 


manent: and other beacons be erect.

CABBAGE CAN BE FOOD: 
‘FOR. KINGS. — 


The homely cabbage is a much 
abuzed, vegetable, to serve it in a 
sodden boiled condition is the 
surest sign of’ careless. catering. 

If. cooked right cabbage can be. 
a, food for kings. Care should be 
taken- never to over-boil the veget- 
able, . to drain carefully, and to 
serve ‘immediately. 

Spanish Way. 
“In Spain they have a way. of 
culting the cabbage into quarters 
and boiling until tender with two 
red peppers, Then it is placed in 
a baking dish with six large 
onions,’ previously boiled, salt’ ‘and 
pepper gidded, w little liquid in 


which the cabbage was cooked ‘pour. |; 


ed over it, and the whole dotted 
with pieces of ‘butter. 

After dusting the vegetables with 
half a teaspoonful of chili’ powder 
the dish is covered and placed in 
a hot oven for half an hour, then 
served very hot.’ 

The Canadian housewife is fond 
of shredding~'the cabbage, with a 
sharp knife, then popping the 
shreds into a pot of boiling water 
which has been salted with about 
a dessertspoonful of salt’ ‘to two" 
quarts of water. 

‘After cooking the: shreds rapidly 
for . not more than ten. minutes, 


she drains throigh a colander and |’ 


‘returns the cabbage.ehreds to the 
pan to keep hot, while she adds o 
of butter, three 
tablespoonfuls of rich icream, and 
a teaspoonful or vinegar. 

Then she turns ‘the delectable | 
concoction into a hot serving dish.: 

A variation isto beat up two 
eggs with the three‘ tablespoonfuls 
of cream, and mix this with 
cooked cabbage.+ It should be put 
into 2 buttered baking’ dish, and 
slipped into the oven to get: nice- 
ly_browned.' 

Tn the Balkans, a cabbage pud- 
ding is made which. is very’ ap- 
petising. For this, drain o head 
of eabbage which has been shredd- 
ed before cooking. . Season, and 
add four tablespoonfuls of bacon 
fat, one cup milk, three beaten 
egies, and two tablespoonfuls or 
wade mustard, 

Pour into a greased baking ‘dish 


_| and cover with: a cupful of sifted
